7 Pendant Lights to Perfect Your Overhead Lighting Scheme (2026)

📅 2020-07-20  |  Read Source →

Sure, recessed lighting serves a functional purpose, but pendant lights are where things get interesting. A quick sweep through any home tour, and you’ll recognize the strategically placed Louis Poulsen above the kitchen island, an Isamu Noguchi in the entryway, and a Herman Miller saucer pendant hovering over the living room will steal the show in any home tour. Below are seven pendant light fixtures that set the mood instead of harshening the vibe. We rounded up the best pendant lights for various design personalities, including a vintage-inspired showpiece and a few variations of low-key ambient lighting. Between them, they cover most of the different types of pendant lights you’re likely to come across, with picks for modern minimalists, Belle Époque enthusiasts, and everyone in between.

First developed in 1958, the PH 5 is a classic for a reason. Renowned Danish lighting manufacturer Louis Poulsen designed the iconic pendant with a three-shade system to equally distribute light. While the pendant works for that functional purpose, it also doubles as an unconventional art piece. Choose from a number of monochromatic and multi-toned colorways, including this steel blue.

Look familiar? That’s because this large pendant’s inspiration, Noguchi’s Akaris, hang in many an AD home tour, including over the dining room table in this Japandi-inspired Bay Area home and in the bedroom of this South African–inspired craft home, for example. There are a few reasons why this style of paper lighting is so popular: It goes with everything; it’s affordable; and it adds a sense of levity to the room with its ethereal shape and its soft, warm glow. As lantern pendants go, this option from Design Within Reach is about as low-commitment as they come. It works anywhere you want ambient lighting rather than a spotlight.

Designer pendant light can cost upwards of $500, but you can pay a fraction of that with finds from Article, a direct-to-consumer furniture store that sells high-quality wares at more palatable prices. That’s certainly the case with the Aino pendant, with matte colorways and a simple angular silhouette that illuminates whateveryou’re cheffing up in your Scandi-style kitchen. The metal cone shade and braided fabric cord make it an easy pick for task lighting over a counter, and if you’re working with a tighter kitchen space, there’s also a mini pendant version for $99 that ships with an LED bulb.

This three-tiered ceiling light from West Elm adds visual texture to blank space, making it a welcome addition to transitional spaces in need of personality. A piece like this can also help to add structure to an open plan where the kitchen and dining rooms collide. This piece comes in 26" and 32" sizes (the larger is a proper large pendant light that can hold its own over a dining table), as well in hardwired and plug-in options, making it one of the few statement hanging lights you can install without calling an electrician.

Any cold kitchen space dominated by stone and steel fixtures could use a touch of handmade whimsy. Hanging this hand-woven rattan pendant over a kitchen island stands to warm up the whole room. Usually, two or three pendant lights hang over a kitchen island; This single shade provides a cozy, welcoming alternative, softening the light rather than spotlighting the countertop, so your mise en place won’t feel so interrogated.

This midcentury Herman Miller pendant, aptly named a saucer for its elliptical shape, is prized by commerce writer Julia Harrison for its retro, Mad Men–esque appeal. Despite the resemblance to our Hay pick above, Miller wasn’t riffing on Noguchi with this pendant: he wanted a Swedish silk-covered pendant he couldn’t justify buying, so he made his own using a sprayed plastic skin originally developed for mothballing Navy ships (whereas the orbital Hay pick uses rice paper). The vertical ribbing gives it a shape that seems to be almost expanding as it hangs. Its white shade and chrome hardware make it easily adaptable to many (if not most) design styles.

This lighting fixture brings to mind 10-foot ceilings, Flemish tapestries, and an echoey marble entryway. If you’re lucky enough to have all of the above, this is exactly the sort of pendant your space needs. If not, luckily, the fixture carries that whole vibe on its own, with its cream glass bowl, brass detailing, and a sculpted metal tassel that adds a bit of movement. A vintage-inspired piece like this transforms a more humble hallway into something dignified and scenic.

October 31, 2019

📅 2020-04-26  |  Read Source →

Album VC, a Lehi, Utah-based early-stage venture firm that’s been known until now as Peak Ventures, just gathered up $75 million in capital commitments for its third early-stage fund.

The development shows investor confidence in Album’s young team, which came together in 2014 after the founder of Zinch — a site connecting colleges and students that sold to the ed-tech company Chegg in 2011 — decided to try his hand at investing in other startups.

As that founder, Sid Krommenhoek — who stayed on with Chegg for several years, running its international efforts — explains it, “I thought I’d do another startup. But I also knew from our own experience in struggling to raise money the need for more capital in Utah, and I thought having that operator-entrepreneur perspective could be useful.”

A first fund, like many in the industry, was more of an experiment, though it was a decent $23 million, thanks largely to the support of Chegg CEO Dan Rosensweig, who was early to commit capital to the fund. A second ($56 million) fund followed when Krommenhoek and John Mayfield — a friend who joined him from a marketing role at Qualtrics — began investing more seriously in their backyard and beyond.

They were soon joined by a third investor, Diogo Myrrha, who began as a principal with the firm and is now a general partner. Explains Krommenhoek of one of the many things that binds the three Brigham Young University grads, “We all came into Utah’s orbit when it wasn’t necessarily a place where you could live out your career, but that’s changed.”

Arranta Bio, a months-old, Watertown, Ma.-based contract development manufacturing organization focused on the biotech industry, has raised $82 million in funding, including from Ampersand Capital Partners and Thermo Fisher. More here.

B8ta, a four-year-old, San Francisco- and New York-based operator of an experiential retail-as-a-service platform, has raised $50 million in Series C funding led by Evolution Ventures, with participation from Macy’s, Khosla Ventures, and Peak State Ventures. Retail Touchpoints has more here.

Tmunity Therapeutics, a four-year-old, Philadelphia, Pa.-based biotherapeutics company trying to propel a series of cancer-fighting cellular therapies through clinical trials, has raised $75 million in Series B funding led by Andreessen Horowitz. Other investors in the round include Kleiner Perkins, Westlake Village BioPartners, Gilead Sciences, The University of Pennsylvania, Be The Match BioTherapies and BrightEdge Venture Fund. More here and here.

Vindex, a months-old, New York-based esports infrastructure platform founded by a former Activision Blizzard vice president, has raised $60 million in Series A funding from unnamed investors. ESPN has more here.

Earlier investors also participated in the round, including Emergence Capital, Mayfield, Cowboy Ventures, and Verizon Ventures. TechCrunch has more here.

October 29, 2019

📅 2020-04-26  |  Read Source →

Chan Park feels like “an eternal nomad.” He has since age 12, when he moved from Korea to the U.S., where he attended middle school in Minnesota, followed by high school in New Jersey, followed by college in New Hampshire. Then he really began to bounce around. Park worked as a trader in New York out of college, embraced the ski-bum life in Utah, then headed to Asia for Uber, where he spent six years, running its expansion team, then managing its entire Southeast Asia business out of Singapore.

More specifically, he was responsible for eight countries across the region, and 350 people, which didn’t give him a lot of time to organize his home. But he wasn’t overwhelmed by the chaos. Instead, he says that in Singapore, something unusual happened. “There’s this huge culture of landlords furnishing space to attract expats,” explains Park. “The furnishings aren’t super high-end, but they’re well-designed and well put together, and it enabled me to be basically moved in as soon as I put my clothes in the closet.” Suddenly, coming home was a treat — a new sensation for him. “For the first time, I was proud to host friends for dinners and barbecues and to just open the door and relax.”

Park knew his U.S. friends could benefit from the same experience, and before long, he was talking with his Dartmouth classmate turned product and industrial designer Christian Talmage about forming their own company. Enter Oliver Space, which provides a lot of what that Singaporean landlord delivered to Park. It furnishes places for busy professionals, making moving into a new home as easy as hanging up their clothes.

The now year-old service is available in the Bay Area only. And Oliver Space employs just a dozen people so far. But the company has already gained enough traction to attract $6.8 million in seed funding from an interesting array of investors, including Mayfield, Abstract Ventures, investors Jana Messerschmidt and April Underwood, Opendoor founder Eric Wu, and Kevin and Julia Hartz of Eventbrite, among others.

Now, Oliver Space just has to grow as quickly, or more so, than other furniture-as-service startups to recently attract funding.

Greensill, an eight-year-old, London-based provider of working capital and supply chain finance, has raised $655 million in new funding from earlier backer SoftBank Vision Fund. Hung founded Medivation, later selling it to Pfizer in a $14 billion deal. He more recently headed to the biotech startup Axovant, where things “went very wrong,” notes FierceBiotech. More here.

Revolution, the investment firm cofounded 14 years ago by entrepreneur-investor Steve Case, has closed its second Rise of the Rest seed fund with $150 million in capital commitments, just like the debut fund it announced two years ago.

Rise of the Rest — which fund startups outside of the biggest U.S. tech hubs in an effort to foster innovation and momentum elsewhere — has rounded up some of the funding from institutional investors, presumably, but also from numerous very wealthy individuals.

According to Forbes, which is currently hosting a summit where Case announced the new fund, some of the new fund’s backers include Jeff Bezos of Amazon, Sara Blakely of Spanx, hedge fund manager Ray Dalio, Under Armour cofounder Kevin Plank, former Tennessee governor Bill Haslam, and Apollo Global Management cofounder Joshua Harris.

Some of these investors backed the first fund, too, including Bezos and Dalio.

Very notably, Hillbilly Elegy author J.D. Vance, who ran the first fund with Case, has stepped back, and longtime Revolution investor David Hall will manage the second fund instead.

Certainly, that first fund kept its investors busy, with stakes in 125 companies. Barely a week passes without a startup announcing some funding from the Rise of the Rest team.

ACV Auctions, a five-year-old, Buffalo, N.Y.-based online platform for franchise and used-car dealerships to buy and sell wholesale inventory through 20-minute auctions, is reportedly looking to raise $150 million in Series E funding at a post-money valuation north of $1 billion. October 25, 2019

📅 2020-04-26  |  Read Source →

And it’s Friday! [Throws frisbee into tree.]

Before we dive into the newsletter, one more bit of great news about our upcoming event (which we promise we’ll stop talking about soon): Michael Grimes, the star tech banker from Morgan Stanley at the center of Uber’s IPO (and Facebook IPO, and Slack’s and Spotify’s), has been persuaded to come and talk about direct listings, which we’re very excited about. If you’re as confused as we are about whether these make sense for more than a couple of companies each year (current status: we don’t think they do), you won’t want to miss this rare conversation with “Wall Street’s Silicon Valley whisperer.”

Note that seats are almost gone. Giant thanks to NextWorld, the early-stage enterprise-focused firm that’s hosting all of us; to KCPR, the boutique tech PR and strategy firm; and to Carta, a platform that helps companies and VCs manage their cap tables, valuations, investments, and equity plans, for their support.

Ben Horowitz,  the co-founder of the venture firm Andreessen Horowitz, has a new book coming out this coming Monday titled “What You Do is Who You Are,” that takes a look at how to create “culture” at a company.

It’s a word that’s thrown around a lot but that’s very hard to grasp, let alone implement in a sustainable way. Horowitz learned firsthand as a CEO how elusive it can be when he took stock of his company, only to discover it was made up of “screamers who intimidated their people,” others who “neglected to give any feedback,” and at least one compulsive liar who excelled at sucking up to Horowitz and also making up stories from whole cloth.

Horowitz says creating culture was a missing part of his education, and in this new book — a follow-up to his best-selling “The Hard Thing About Hard Things” — he does his best to fill that gap for other CEOs, using his own experience, as well as lessons gleaned from historical figures Toussaint Louverture and Genghis Khan, along with Shaka Senghor, a contemporary who served time for murder and today is a criminal justice reform advocate.

It’s an instructive and novel combination, and we suggest picking up the book, especially if you love history. In the meantime, we sat down recently with Horowitz to talk about its timing and whether some of the biggest cultural blow-ups in the startup industry — Uber and WeWork — could have been avoided. These excerpts have been edited for length and clarity. Note that we’ll have more of the conversation — including Horowitz’s thoughts about dual-class shares —  for readers of Extra Crunch on Monday.

You’ve just written a book about culture that’s coming out just as a lot of questions are being raised about culture because of WeWork. What happened there?

[Cofounder Adam Neumann] had a certain kind of culture there. He had some holes — some great strengths and great holes. And sometimes that happens. When you’re really good at part of it, you can delude yourself into thinking that you’ve got everything you need when you have some massive incompleteness.

Adam is so amazing. Like, the way they got all the money and everything. And the vision was so spectacular. And everybody there believed it, and they recruited some phenomenal talent. But when you’re that optimistic, it does help to have something in the culture that says [allows] people to bring you the bad news, like, if the accounting is all over the place or what have you.

As with Uber’s Travis Kalanick, whose culture also came under fire, Neumann operated in very plain sight. He wasn’t hiding who he was or what he was spending.

Right, everybody knew how Travis was running the company. Everyone in Silicon Valley knew, let alone everyone on the board. The culture was published. You can look up Uber’s values [from that period].

Travis designed, I think, a really compelling culture, and believed in it, and published it. And the consequences of what he was missing were also super well-known. It’s only when board members think people are coming after them that [they take an interest in these things].

What are the biggest lessons in these two cases?

I obviously know more about Uber [as a Lyft investor who follows the space]. In Uber’s case, it’s a very subtle thing. Travis had a really good code. But he had a bug in it.

I think it was reported that, like, Travis encouraged bad behavior. I don’t think he did at all. I just think he didn’t make it clear that legal and ethical [considerations] were more important than competitiveness. As a result, when left to their own devices, in a distributed organization where there was a lot of distributed power, that combination had people doing things that were out of bounds.

And he was making everybody so much money. And the company was growing so fast that, for the board members, I suspect they were like, ‘As long as it’s making money, I’m not going to worry about what happens next.’

To me, the unfair part is, like, they shouldn’t get any credit at the end. Whatever you’re blaming Travis for [you should blame them, too] because they didn’t see it, either. I think that’s a charitable way of putting it.

The 9 Best Binoculars, According to Our Tests and Research

📅 2019-10-18  |  Read Source →

See the world in greater detail with one of these recommended models.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

Despite ubiquitous satellite imagery, incredible smartphone cameras, and a video doorbell on almost every house, there’s still incredible power in witnessing something with your own eyes. Unfortunately, despite eyesight being our dominant sense, our optics have some real limitations. Binoculars are considered relatively old technology, but they’re still recognized as the best tool to reach for when identifying a far-off animal or examining details on a backyard bird.

Hunters use binoculars to spot and pick out details on their quarry thousands of yards away, birders confirm species they’re identifying, and stargazers zoom in on constellations. They’re also just handy to keep in your house or even your vehicle for those moments when you just want to “see for yourself.”

You can extend your vision with telescopes, spotting scopes, and even zoom camera lenses, but the comfort and ease of use are why binoculars have such enduring appeal. Monocular devices—spotting scopes, for instance—can put strain on your eyes after only a few minutes, while binoculars offer a much more natural extension of normal vision, making them more comfortable for long sessions and easier for beginners to use. Binoculars are also lighter than those similar devices, which makes them packable and portable for trips into nature where they offer a more intimate look at shy creatures.

The challenge in shopping for a pair of binoculars is the sheer number and diversity of options from big, high-powered zooms that require a tripod to pocket-sized budget versions and everything in between. To help you narrow your search, we dive into how to select a pair that fits within your price range and how you intend to use them. We also share our favorites from years of testing in the field.

I’m a lifelong hunter and wildlife enthusiast who uses binoculars year-round for hunting as well as volunteer wildlife surveys for bighorn sheep and goats. Long hours behind glass, as well as more than a decade of testing dozens of pairs of binoculars in the field for editorial reviews, have informed my picks, along with conversations with brand representatives and optics dealers. For this update of my long-running guide, I tested six new pairs of binoculars that are new releases from established optics brands. Not all of them made the cut here, but several did, including the Nikon Trailblazer II 10x25, the first time I enjoyed a microcompact binocular enough to include it.

The new Leupold BX-3 Alpine HD binoculars deliver premium-enough outdoor optics while maintaining a price point almost everyone can stomach. The BX-2 Alpine HD, which occupied that exact niche for years, did it well enough that it became my favorite binocular in the midrange category and my everyday hunting optic.

This is a compact, lightweight binocular with good clarity and low-light performance, which makes it a solid choice for basically everyone from birders to stargazers, even though Leupold tends to market mostly to hunters and target shooters. While not the cheapest bino on the market, it’s worth the upgrade over the lowest-tier optics and, like any Leupold optic, comes with a lifetime warranty, meaning it could conceivably be the last pair you’ll ever buy.

The BX-3 doesn’t reinvent anything, but Leupold subtly refined the binos, updating coatings, tweaking ergonomics, and adding a few quality-of-life improvements while keeping the same general form factor, magnification options, and modest price point that made the BX-2 so successful in the first place.

The price jumped up slightly with the release of the BX-3, so while I’d buy the BX-3 for the improvements if you’re paying full price, retailers appear to be deeply discounting the existing stock of BX-2s, so it’s worth considering the older model if there’s a great price on it, at least until retailers run out of the now-discontinued model.

Read the Full Review of the Leupold BX-3 Here

Zeiss is synonymous with good glass in consumer camera lenses and sport optics, so the mid-tier-priced Terra ED 10x42 binoculars are somewhat of a surprise in the manufacturer’s lineup. I’ve tested several higher-end Zeiss binoculars, which were unsurprisingly great given their price tags, but finding nearly as good clarity and color in this $500 pair was unexpected.

While $500 isn’t cheap for most casual binocular buyers, I’d argue getting Zeiss quality at this price presents a serious value if you’re looking for good glass but aren’t ready to put down a mortgage payment to get it.

The 10x42 Terra ED uses ED glass and Zeiss's hydrophobic multicoating to produce a bright, high-contrast image that will feel like a serious upgrade to budget options. It's not going to match the edge-to-edge perfection or low-light performance of Zeiss's flagship binoculars, but it delivers enough optical quality to be an obvious jump in clarity for the investment.

The 10x42 configuration is a versatile middle ground for hunting, hiking, birding, and general wildlife observation. The Terra ED has a 330-foot field of view at 1,000 yards and a 5.3-foot close focus, so it is adequate for birding, casual stargazing, and wildlife watching. At 25.6 ounces, it's also light enough to carry comfortably in a chest harness (not included) for long stretches without pulling on your neck.

The fiberglass-reinforced housing is waterproof and nitrogen-filled to minimize fog and condensation outdoors. The hydrophobic coating helps keep moisture from becoming a major distraction when you're using the binoculars in wet weather or going between temperature extremes. The large focus wheel is also easy to find and operate quickly.

The Terra ED isn't the cheapest 10x42 on the market, but that's not really the point. It's a relatively affordable way into a serious optics brand, and the difference is visible when you use them side by side with cheaper binos.

Most hunters don't need the biggest or most expensive binoculars they can buy, but many with the budget and obsession with the sport will spend thousands hoping expensive optics can buy success. While $3,000 binoculars are overkill for most, hunters do ask a lot of their optics. Early morning and late evening light and long distances expose the weaknesses of lower-quality glass, so many hunters get the advice to buy the best glass they can afford.

While the top-tier binoculars are undoubtedly better, there’s a mid-tier sweet spot that gives you most of the performance of those four-figure binos at a fraction of the price. The Vortex Viper HD 10x42 binoculars are squarely there. Vortex’s Triumph binoculars are our Best Budget pick above for the value they provide at $100, but dedicated hunters who get up early and stay out late will eventually want to upgrade.

The Viper HD 10x42s have a list price around $729, but can be found at most retailers for closer to $500, a good value for the upgrade. Still, that’s $400 more than the Triumph, so you’ll want to be sure you value the improvements. The benefits over cheaper options are better light transmission and reduced distortions such as chromatic aberrations. If you’ve used cheaper binos, you’ve seen off colors such as purple fringes on trees. In broad daylight, this isn’t a problem you can’t adjust your eyes to, but in low light and longer distances it can result in straining and struggling to tell a stump from a bear or deer.

A relatively low 24.9-ounce weight and compact form factor enabled by the roof prism design make these comfortable to carry in a chest harness over long days in the field or a tree stand. The rubber housing offers satisfactory grip and I notably appreciated the underside thumb grooves, though they don’t look as high-end as magnesium housings. The included chest harness is capable enough, but will be a throwaway for hunters who already own a specialized hunting chest rig.

Turned off by cheap compact binoculars in the past? These are worth it. They offer quality optics with powerful-enough magnification for bird-watching, hunting, and a range of everyday uses. In addition, they’re sealed, so they're waterproof and fogproof, and there is a lifetime warranty on the optical parts so you won’t need to replace them if you drop these binos even once.

Nikon’s Trailblazer II 10x25 compact binoculars make some compromises on field of view and low-light performance, but on the plus side, they’re small enough to fit in your pocket. These are binoculars you can toss in a daypack for when you want them, rather than committing to positioning them on you, front and center, and dealing with the weight.

The trade-off, however, is the 25mm objective lens. There's simply less glass available to gather light than you'll get with a 42mm binocular, and the field of view isn’t bad but feels cramped if you’re used to bigger binos. The 10mm eye relief also isn't particularly generous for eyeglass wearers. But for quick looks during daylight, the compact size makes these much easier to have on you when you need them.

Bigger is typically better when it comes to optical performance in binoculars, but there is always a give-and-take. With standard 10x42 or larger binoculars, you get better performance, but usually at least a pound of weight and a bulky item. Bird-watchers and hunters use chest harnesses to distribute the weight and keep them accessible, but that’s a commitment you don’t always want for a casual hike.

The Trailblazer II has a more palatable price at $130 than you’ll find with larger binos with similar optical quality, making these a no-brainer if you’re shopping for casual use.

Like Leupold, Vortex has a great reputation, in large part due to its accommodating lifetime warranty program—a comfort to those of us who are hard on our gear. One challenge with Vortex is the sheer number of lines and models, but the newer Triumph line is easy to understand: it’s the brand’s cheapest pair of binoculars in the 10x42 build and arguably its best value at $100. While the optical clarity and quality aren’t on par with that of the more expensive lines, the Triumph does surprisingly well, especially in daylight.

Like most cheaper binos, the clarity falls off the closer you get to the edges of the field of view, but is sharp and color-true in the center. Yes, you can spend less, but I wouldn’t recommend it, having tried a lot of $25 to $50 binoculars over the years. Despite the attractive price, the Triumph ships with a case, harness, and neck strap as well.

Full Vortex Triumph 10x42 Binoculars Review

Nikon is best known for its cameras and lenses, but this Japanese optics brand makes a wide range of excellent binoculars as well. I’ve tested several models over the years, but the Monarch M7 stands out for the optical clarity it delivers at a mid-tier price point.

Nearly any pair of binoculars will work for birding, but for most species, you’ll be in trees and fairly close quarters where higher magnifications can make it harder to spot and track birds with the more zoomed-in view. Even when you spot a bird, even the slightly tighter 10x magnification can make it harder to follow flight.

The 8x42 version of the Monarch M7 binos gives you enough magnification to confirm details without spooking wildlife, but the wide field makes it easier to track them. The M5 series is also good and about $200 cheaper, but it has a narrow field of view even in the 8x42 configuration.

Like most binoculars, they’re nitrogen-purged and O-ring sealed, which just means they’re waterproof and fogproof. (Note: Fogproof only means there won’t be interior fog. You can still steam up the glass if, for example, your face is warm and the air or binos are cold.)

More money can, of course, get you even better glass from Nikon and other companies, but this is one of the best mid-tier options that delivers a clear and comfortable birding experience. Less money will downgrade your clarity, but cheaper and smaller options that weigh and cost much less abound if those are your priorities. (The entry-level Nocs Provisions Standard Issue 8x25 binos, for example, weigh about half as much.)

There are times when 10x binoculars simply aren't enough—and that’s especially true in Western hunting country where the animal you're looking for might be thousands of yards across a canyon. A 15x binocular like Maven's C.4 gives you considerably more reach while retaining the depth perception and comfort of looking through both eyes.

Extra reach is the reason to buy these. At 15x, details that can be frustratingly ambiguous through a conventional 10x42 become much easier to pick apart. I've found high-power binoculars particularly useful for searching large expanses of terrain where the ability to use both eyes makes extended glassing considerably more comfortable than squinting through a spotting scope. At distances of a mile or more, you’ll still want a spotter that’s 20x and up, but the 15x is a meaningful middle ground that’s wide enough to use in a forest, but reaches far enough for big mountain hunting.

The C.4 combines 15x magnification with large 56mm objective lenses, giving it a 215-foot field of view at 1,000 yards. This is noticeably tighter than, for example, the 342-foot field of view of the Leupold BX-3 10x42s, but that’s by design and the trade-off for 50 percent more zoom.

The other big trade-off is the weight and size. At 45 ounces, they’re still lighter than a spotting scope, but you’ll likely also be carrying a tripod, which adds several pounds to your pack. Compare this to 10x42s, which are usually 1 to 2 pounds total, and it’s a big difference. Still, if you’re using binoculars to cover big mountain distances and need the clarity and zoom to identify animals precisely, it’s likely a weight penalty you’ll be willing to pay.

The ED glass and phase-corrected, dielectric-coated prisms produce a surprisingly sharp image, and the 92 percent light transmission is key around dawn and dusk—impressive for the price. Most high-quality, high-power binoculars are at least four figures, but Maven delivers the C.4 for $550. Unlike the rest of its line that uses Japanese and Chinese components that are assembled in Wyoming, the C.4 is produced entirely in China. Despite this difference, I didn’t notice a sharp drop-off in build or optical quality from other Maven binos I’ve tested.

There's a catch: These aren't really handheld binoculars. At 45.1 ounces, the C.4 is almost twice the weight of many conventional 10x42s, and 15x magnification makes even small hand movements obvious. I used the C.4 handheld fine for short periods, but the size and weight wear on your stability much faster than lightweight hunting binos. Once mounted, however, the extra weight only affects your pack and the C.4 becomes a remarkably capable long-distance optic.

Any decent pair of binoculars can enhance casual stargazing, but if you’re closer to “astrophile” than “occasional noticer of the night sky,” you’ll want a somewhat specialized pair and the best low-light performance you can afford. As binoculars go up in price, into the thousands, the returns are diminishing, giving you small increments of improved clarity and edge-case benefits, but they are better.

Celestron is a budget binocular brand, but its SkyMaster series is astro-focused and worth a look if you’re focused on stars. The massive objective lenses, high magnification, and Porro prism combine for a heavy build, but one that’s designed for night use at the distance of light-years.

The 25x magnification won’t be much good for backyard bird-watching or any handheld use, but they’re designed for tripod mounting, and for use as a stationary sky-watching setup that’s more comfortable for longer sessions than telescopes.

Going with binoculars for sky watching, especially ones as zoomed-in as these, adds a layer of dimensionality that’s lost looking with the naked eye (or with one eye as with telescopes). Unlike cheap Amazon optics brands, Celestron has friendly price points but still gives you a lifetime warranty. If your stargazing habit sticks, you may eventually want to upgrade to a higher-end model, but this pair will show you what you’ve been missing for a few hundred bucks.

Any decent pair of binoculars can enhance casual stargazing, but if you’re closer to “astrophile” than “occasional noticer of the night sky,” you’ll want a somewhat specialized pair and the best low-light performance you can afford. As binoculars go up in price, into the thousands, the returns are diminishing, giving you small increments of improved clarity and edge-case benefits, but they are better.

Celestron is a budget binocular brand, but its SkyMaster series is astro-focused and worth a look if you’re focused on stars. The massive objective lenses, high magnification, and Porro prism combine for a heavy build, but one that’s designed for night use at the distance of light-years.

The 25x magnification won’t be much good for backyard bird-watching or any handheld use, but they’re designed for tripod mounting, and for use as a stationary sky-watching setup that’s more comfortable for longer sessions than telescopes.

Going with binoculars for sky watching, especially ones as zoomed-in as these, adds a layer of dimensionality that’s lost looking with the naked eye (or with one eye as with telescopes). Unlike cheap Amazon optics brands, Celestron has friendly price points but still gives you a lifetime warranty. If your stargazing habit sticks, you may eventually want to upgrade to a higher-end model, but this pair will show you what you’ve been missing for a few hundred bucks.

Yes, this is an article about binoculars, but I humbly present the little-known monocular as a better tool for certain jobs. Like spotting scopes, monoculars aren’t as natural to use since you have to close one eye and miss out on the depth of a stereo picture. However, there are times when the weight and bulk of binos mean you leave them at home even though you might want them in the field.

I personally carry this Nocs Field Tube monocular every time I go backcountry skiing and find it quite literally worth its weight for scouting potential approach and descent lines, using it to pick out details about snow quality, avalanches, and terrain that my naked eye regularly misses. It’s also easy to throw in a pack or pocket for casual hikes or pre-hunting season scouting walks where I never know when I might want a closer look at something but am not motivated enough to harness up with my binoculars.

There aren’t a lot of companies making monoculars, but Nocs has several options. I love the 8x32 size of the Field Tube, but you can get a 10x32 version that offers a bit more zoom but a slightly narrower field of view in the same size package. There’s also the newer 8x32 Zoom Tube that’s significantly cheaper and slightly lighter and smaller but isn’t fully waterproof.

Not everything we test or like ends up in our buying guide, but some are products that still warrant a mention. Here are a few.

Vortex Crossfire HD 10x42: These budget binos fall between the Triumph and Viper models we recommend above. They’re solid optics for the cost, but in a no-man’s-land for the price. If you’re on a tight budget, the Triumph 10x42s are less than half the price and nearly as good. And if you’re looking to upgrade to the mid-tier, it’s worth saving up for the Vipers or the Zeiss Terra ED 10x42s for a more noticeable jump in quality in a compact all-arounder.Nocs Field Issue 10x42: Give us some fun colors and a grippy, overmolded texture like on these 10x42s. But similar to the Vortex Crossfire, they’re not enough of an improvement on our best budget pick to justify double the cost. If you like the design that stands out from a crowd of bland black options, you won’t be disappointed, but we prefer to pay for performance.Zeiss SFL 12x50: These are awesome high-end binoculars that pack a little extra zoom as well as a large, 50mm objective lens that pulls in extra light for early and late. They also are incredibly compact and light despite their specs. The problem is the $2,000 price tag that puts them out of reach of most casual users. They’re really good, just not 10 times as good as a solid $200 pair from our list above.

The magnifications of most consumer binoculars are fixed (i.e., not zoomable), making it one of the most important decisions for buyers. More isn't always better and can feel claustrophobic in tight quarters such as trying to track a small bird in dense forest, while wider-angle, lower-power lenses can feel underpowered if you're trying to see across longer distances.

Most binos range between 7x and 18x. Much less than 7x isn’t enough of an effect to bother with for most applications, while 15x and up becomes difficult to use without a tripod. In general, lower-power binoculars are a better fit if you’re mostly glassing shorter distances, such as in forests, and don’t need precise detail. Higher-power binoculars make sense if you’re looking to avoid also carrying a spotting scope and glassing longer distances up to a mile or need precise detail for identifying wildlife.

Objective lens diameter is the size of the lens farthest from your eyes and impacts the field of vision you get, as well as how bright the image is since, generally, bigger lenses capture more light. Jeremy Bentham, senior manager of SRO Sales & Operations for Nikon, explains the trade-offs associated with objective lens size.

“One of the key things that needs to be decided is brightness versus weight,” he says. “Larger objective lenses allow more light into the optic but are also heavier to pack and carry with you.”

Objective lens sizes in binoculars range anywhere from 20 mm to 100 mm, but the common sizes are mostly in the 30 mm to 60 mm range. I find ultracompact binoculars of less than about 30 mm annoying to use for more than a quick look at something nearby since the field of view is small and it can be harder to keep in position on your eyes. On the other end of the spectrum, most binoculars larger than about 55 mm become heavy and unwieldy if used without a tripod.

Binoculars are listed by size spec, where the first number is magnification and the second is objective lens diameter. Probably the most common size is 10x42, which permits a big enough field of view and magnification for viewing things as near as 15 feet but still allows you to spot larger objects such as wildlife as far as a mile away.

For backcountry hunters and hikers, weight is one of the most important considerations in a binocular. You need to balance the desire for a larger, brighter optic with your ability and interest to carry it around your neck over long distances. For me, anything heavier than about 35 ounces is too much to carry in a chest harness when actively hunting and covering several miles a day. If you’re a hunter or hiker who doesn’t need ready access to your binoculars and can keep them in a pack until you need them at specific spots, you may be able to tolerate heavier options.

Even folks who mostly use binoculars at home or from a vehicle need to consider weight. Heavier binoculars are harder to use for longer periods of time since your arms and shoulders do eventually fatigue and become shaky. If you’re mostly using binoculars on a tripod (a must for higher-magnification binos), you may want to prize optical quality over weight savings.

Aside from range-finding binoculars, most have surprisingly little tech as we usually think of it in terms of electronics and connectivity. What makes great binoculars hasn’t changed much over the years and still boils down to quality glass and craftsmanship.

Where tech does show up is in the glass quality and the coatings used. High-tech coatings are used to make colors true, enhance brightness, and protect your expensive lenses. Coatings and glass often use nonstandardized terms such as ED, HD, and UHD glass, or proprietary branding such as LotuTec or Swarobright, making it hard to compare apples to apples when talking about something as subjective as optical quality. What you can look for is whether lenses are “fully multi-coated,” meaning that all glass surfaces have multiple coatings for durability and performance, whatever the branding.

Jamie, Senior Reviews Editor, joined the Hearst Enthusiast Group in 2021. She has covered technology and consumer lifestyle gadgets since 1995—and shared her expertise in print, digital, and in broadcast originating the role of The Gizmo Girl for ESPN’s Cold Pizza. She has written about, tested, and reviewed everything from turkey fryers to high-definition TVs. Her byline has appeared in TWICE, Sound & Vision, Consumer Reports, and many others. In her free time, Jamie is in a yoga class, searching for the perfect matcha latte, or walking the boards.

How to Clean Your Camping and Backpacking Tent

Turkey Pot Pie

📅 2019-10-02  |  Read Source →

Chicken pot pie is a perfect cozy dinner for chilly fall days, but like most classics, it could use an upgrade. This turkey pot pie is a perfect way to switch up your meal rotation and make sure those Thanksgiving turkey leftovers don’t go to waste. That being said, this dinner is so comforting, it deserves to be made all season long.

What People Are Saying:“This was AMAZING. The filling is delicious, the crust is super flaky and golden brown. I will make this again for sure.” - GoldTeepee

Pop your butter in the freezer for about 15 minutes before you start making the crust so it’s adequately cold. Grab a large food processor and pulse together the flour, sugar, and salt until they’re mixed. Toss in the butter and pulse again until you see pea-sized pieces and larger chunks.

With the motor running, add vinegar and four tablespoons of ice water, one tablespoon at a time, until the dough just comes together and becomes moist. Make sure the dough isn’t wet and sticky or too crumbly (you can test this by squeezing some with your fingers). If the dough is too crumbly and not coming together, add more ice water, 1 teaspoon at a time.

Turn the dough out onto a lightly floured surface and gently squeeze it together. Knead it just a couple of times so it comes together, then split it into two balls. Flatten each one into a smooth disk, wrap them up in plastic, and pop them in the fridge for at least an hour or even overnight if you want to get ahead.

Move your oven rack to the center, then preheat to 375°. Grab a big, deep skillet and melt the butter over medium heat. Toss in the onions and carrots, and stir until they’re soft. Give it about 7 to 8 minutes. Next, add the garlic and cook, stirring, just until it smells amazing (about a minute). Now, grab a spatula or wooden spoon and stir in the flour. Cook, scraping up any tasty bits from the bottom, until the mixture turns golden and starts to bubble—just 2 or 3 minutes.

Pour the wine in slowly, stirring as you go. Let it simmer for about 3 minutes, just until that alcohol smell is gone. Then, slowly add 1 1/3 cups of cream, still scraping up all the good stuff from the bottom. Keep cooking until the mixture starts to boil and thickens up a bit. This should take around 5 minutes.

Fold in the turkey, peas, thyme, and parsley. Give it all a good season with kosher salt and pepper. Take the skillet off the heat and let the filling cool until you’re ready to use it.

Butter up a 9" pie dish or a deep skillet for your crust. On a lightly floured surface, roll out one disk of dough into a circle about 16" across and 1/4" thick. Carefully lay the dough into the pie dish, letting the edges slump down naturally. Trim the dough, leaving roughly a 1" overhang. Finally, pour in the cooled filling.

Roll out the second disk of dough into a 16"-diameter round about 1/4" thick and arrange it on top of the filling. Fold the top overhang into the bottom, pressing firmly to seal. Crimp the edges if you’d like. Using a paring knife, cut slits on top, and brush with the remaining 2 tablespoons of cream and sprinkle with sea salt.

Bake the pie (cover the edges with foil if they start getting too brown) until the crust is golden and the filling is bubbling. This takes about 1 hour 15 minutes. Once it’s done, move the pie to a wire rack and let it cool for about 15 minutes before digging in.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Cover your pot pie tightly with plastic wrap or store in an airtight container in the fridge for up to five days after baking. If you’d like to make your pot pie ahead of time and freeze it, wrap the whole (baked or unbaked) turkey pot pie tightly in plastic wrap and store in the freezer for up to 2 months.

Dry-Brined Turkey With Tangy Honey Glaze

📅 2018-10-16  |  Read Source →

This dry-brine turkey recipe produces a Thanksgiving centerpiece that’s full of flavor and juicy as heck. The brine is just kosher salt and brown sugar, applied at least 12 hours and up to 2 days ahead. The salt helps the meat retain moisture, while an uncovered rest in the fridge dries the skin for better browning—no bucket required.

Roasting the bird on a wire rack set inside a rimmed baking sheet (instead of a high-sided roasting pan) lets hot air circulate around it, encouraging browning all over. We’ve also done away with the turkey baster in favor of a buttery honey-vinegar glaze you’ll brush over the bird while it cooks. Fragrant with rosemary, garlic, and orange zest, with Worcestershire sauce for savory depth, the repeated brushing builds an even, glossy lacquer.

Let the cooked turkey rest uncovered for 30 minutes to 1 hour before carving. Resist the urge to tent it with foil, which traps steam and softens that hard-earned crispy skin. Use the downtime to put the finishing touches on your Thanksgiving side dishes.

What kind of turkey should I buy for dry-brining?

Choose an unseasoned turkey. Kosher, self-basting, and enhanced turkeys have already been salted, so adding this dry brine can make them overly salty. Check the label for an added salt solution before buying. A frozen turkey is fine; thaw it in the refrigerator for at least 24 hours before starting the brine.

No. In this recipe, the brine stays on the bird; Step 2 calls for rinsing the baking sheet and rack to prevent the drippings from becoming too salty, not the turkey. Follow the measurement for your brand of kosher salt, and don’t feel compelled to use every last grain of the mixture—the recipe intentionally makes extra.

Yes, but taste them first: The dry brine and glaze make these drippings especially flavorful, and they may be quite salty. Skim off the excess fat to make your roux, then add the juices a little at a time, followed by low-sodium chicken broth (or homemade unseasoned turkey stock).

Our Make-Ahead Gravy doesn’t need drippings, but you can stir some in as it reheats. Taste before adding salt.

Can I use a larger or spatchcocked turkey?

Yes, but not with the same roasting schedule. This dry brine recipe is written for a whole 12–14-lb. bird; a larger turkey or one that’s been flattened will cook differently. For a spatchcocked bird, follow our Spatchcock Turkey With Anise and Orange rather than transferring this recipe’s roasting and glazing times.

See why each step works in our guide to cooking Thanksgiving turkey →

4 hours 45 minutes, plus at least 12 hours brining

Place ½ cup Diamond Crystal or ¼ cup plus 1½ tsp. Morton kosher salt and 1 Tbsp. light brown sugar in a small bowl and work together with your fingers until incorporated. Place one 12–14-lb. turkey, neck reserved for gravy, giblets discarded, on a wire rack set inside a rimmed baking sheet and pat dry with paper towels. (If you don’t have this setup, place your turkey on a V-shape rack set inside a large roasting pan.) Sprinkle salt mixture all over outside of the turkey as well as inside the cavity of the turkey, patting to adhere and nudging some into every crevice. You won’t need all of the dry brine, but it’s good to have extra since some of it will end up on the baking sheet as you season the turkey. Chill bird, uncovered, at least 12 hours and up to 2 days.

Remove turkey from wire rack and rinse baking sheet and rack if needed (turkey will most likely release some liquid onto pan). Line baking sheet with 3 layers of foil and set rack back inside. Place turkey, breast side up, on rack and tuck wings underneath. Let turkey sit at room temperature 2–3 hours.

Place an oven rack in middle of oven; preheat oven to 450°. Using your fingers, loosen turkey skin on breast. Work 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature, under skin, spreading evenly over both breasts. Smear outside of turkey with another 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature.

Tie legs together with kitchen twine and pour 1 cup water into baking sheet.

Roast turkey, rotating pan halfway through, until skin is mostly golden brown all over, about 30 minutes.

Meanwhile, cook ¼ cup sherry vinegar or red wine vinegar, 2 Tbsp. honey, 4 tsp. Worcestershire sauce, 3 sprigs rosemary, 3 garlic cloves, crushed, two 2x1" strips orange zest, and remaining 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature, in a small saucepan over medium heat until bubbling and slightly thickened, about 5 minutes. Reduce heat to lowest setting and keep glaze warm.

Reduce oven temperature to 300° and continue to roast turkey, brushing with glaze every 30 minutes and adding more water by ½-cupfuls as needed to maintain some liquid in baking sheet, until an instant-read thermometer inserted into the thickest part of breast near the neck registers 155° (don’t worry; the temperature will continue to climb while the bird rests), 65–85 minutes longer. Skin should be deep golden brown, shiny, and crisp. Transfer turkey to a cutting board and let rest at least 30 minutes and up to 1 hour before carving.

Editor’s note: The dry-brine turkey recipe was first printed in our November 2018 issue as ‘Dry-Rubbed Roast Turkey.’ Head this way for more of our best Thanksgiving recipes →

Dutch Apple Pie

📅 2018-08-22  |  Read Source →

Dutch apple pie is the upgrade the fall classic needs. With a nutty, streusel topping, this cozy dessert will have you coming back for slice after slice. It’s also easier to make than the traditional version because there’s no need to cook the apple filling ahead of time. In fact, some of us will be swapping out our traditional apple pie recipe for this one for Thanksgiving this year.

Lightly flour your work surface, then roll out the chilled dough into a 12-inch circle. Carefully move it to a 9-inch pie dish. Gently lift the edges and let the dough settle into the pan. Make sure not to stretch the dough. Press it firmly into the bottom and sides. Pop it in the fridge until it’s cold (about 30 minutes), or freeze it for 10 minutes if you’re in a hurry.

Grab a medium bowl and whisk together the granulated sugar, flour, cinnamon, and salt.

In a large bowl, whisk together the egg, sour cream, and vanilla. Add the dry ingredients and whisk until the mixture is nice and smooth.

Move a rack to the center of your oven and preheat it to 350°. In a medium bowl, whisk together the brown sugar, flour, granulated sugar, walnuts, oats, and cinnamon. Add the butter and use a fork or pastry cutter to work it in until crumbly.

Spread the apples evenly in your prepared pie crust. Pour the filling over the apples, then sprinkle the crumble topping all over.

Bake the pie until the crust and crumble are golden brown and you don’t see any more moisture. This should take about an hour. Once it’s done, let the pie cool on a wire rack until it reaches room temperature, then pop it in the fridge for an hour before serving.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Because this pie has a sour cream and egg-based filling, store leftovers covered in the refrigerator for up to 4 days.

The 8 Best Cordless Stick Vacuums Are Convenient, Versatile, and Work Well on Carpets Too

📅 2018-08-03  |  Read Source →

These lightweight, easy-to-store models get the job done with multiple attachments and powerful motors.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

A cordless stick vacuum has the potential to be one of the most convenient cleaning tools in your home. These lightweight machines make it easy to clean up pet hair from area rugs and hidden dust along baseboards, without having to haul out an upright vacuum or wrestling with a power cord. Most models also convert into handheld vacuums, giving you more flexibility to clean furniture, stairs, and other hard-to-reach spots.

Suction power, battery life, maneuverability, and dustbin capacities vary between models, so the right cordless stick vacuum ultimately depends on your home’s flooring, how much debris you’re dealing with, and the features that will actually make your cleaning routine easier.

We’ve rounded up the best cordless stick vacuums worth considering based on our hands-on experience, with options for everything from everyday maintenance to deeper carpet cleaning.

🔎 Looking for more ways to keep your floors and furniture clean? Check out our stories on the best robot vacuums, the best vacuums for pet hair, the best vacuums for hardwood floors, and the best handheld vacuums.

I’ve spent considerable time researching, testing, and vetting all types of vacuums, so I’ve developed a pretty good understanding of what separates a genuinely useful appliance from one that is just going to take up space in your storage closet. I’ve evaluated a wide range of models, including full-size uprights, canister vacuums, robot, and handheld models vacuums, paying close attention to performance, ease of use, and features that can really make a difference in a your regular cleaning routine.

When testing cordless stick vacuums, we assessed how well each model could handle common household debris, its ability to transition between carpet and hardwood floors, and how easy it was to move around furniture and into tighter spaces. We also looked at the practical considerations like battery life, suction power, dustbin capacity, weight, filtration, and included attachments.

Beyond just how the vacuum cleans, we also considered the overall experience for an owner. Removable batteries, self-cleaning docking stations, washable filters, and easy-to-empty dustbins can also make daily vacuuming less of a drag. The below recommendations are both powerful and dependable for a wide range of cleaning needs and surface types.

The Dyson V15 Detect is the most expensive stick vacuum in our lineup, but if you can stomach the price tag, you’ll get a cordless stick vacuum that (quite literally) leaves the competition in the dust across the board. It delivers top-notch performance on floors and carpets to tackle everything from fine dust and pet dander to larger debris.

Its motor spins up to 125,000 rpm and it comes with two cleaning heads, including an upgraded version of the brand’s “fluffy” head that features a green laser headlight to detect fine dust. During testing, we found that being able to switch from cordless to handheld made it easier to transition from tackling small messes to deeper cleaning sessions.

The auto mode is a nice feature for conserving battery life, as this model only runs for about an hour off one charge. Another highlight is the motorized pet tool.

Not every cordless stick vacuum we test ends up in this roundup. However, these other models still perform well and are reliable recommendations:

Levoit Aero Cordless Vacuum Cleaner: The Tineco we’ve included above has more raw suction power, reaching up to 27,000Pa versus the 14,000Pa of the Levoit. However, one of the highlights of the Aero is the 3.5-liter-capacity dust bag in the docking station. Depending on your cleaning frequency, you could potentially go months without having to empty and replace it, and this design is better for keeping dust and dander from being expelled back into the air.

Dyson Gen5Detect Cordless Stick Vacuum: This cordless stick vacuum certainly commands a premium price, and you get a lot of the same features as the Dyson V15 Detect we recommend above, such as an LCD display screen, the Fluffy Optic cleaner head, and pet hair attachments. If you’re an allergy sufferer, you may prefer the Gen5Detect because its filtration system captures particles down to 0.1 microns, while the V15 filters down to just 0.3 microns. Plus, runtime is slightly longer on the Gen5Detect.

The main advantages of owning a cordless stick vacuum are easy portability and storage. But what you trade for the convenience is battery life and runtime. Most models clean from 30 to 60 minutes, but that’s also dependent on what setting you’re using. If you’re running the vacuum on its highest speed the entire time, it might die faster. Be wary of manufacturers’ advertised total runtime because it may represent performance on the lowest setting or using two fully charged batteries, for instance. Battery charging should also be factored in, so plan your cleaning session accordingly so you don’t lose power in the middle of a floor swipe.

Like we mentioned earlier, the dustbins on cordless stick vacuums are significantly smaller than those of an upright or canister unit. You should consider your cleaning habits and the spaces you usually vacuum to find a model that aligns with your needs. If you live in an apartment or are using the cordless vac to supplement your other cleaning supplies, then a dustbin with at least half a liter capacity should do. Some of the models we recommend hold up to one liter of debris, which helps limit the number of trips to the trash can.

Although not a necessity, having a good amount of attachments with your vacuum is especially helpful for cleaning hard-to-reach places or targeting specific issues. Crevice tools pick up dirt and dander from corners and couch cushions, while bristled brushes and motorized pet tools agitate and lift hair and fur that’s been embedded into upholstery.

A good filtration system helps combat allergens such as dust and pet dander that are being kicked up by a vacuum. The best option is a HEPA filter, or “high-efficiency particulate air” filter, which traps 99.97 percent of particles that are 0.3 microns or larger in size. A fully sealed or multi-step filtration system also has the potential to trap a wide variety of airborne allergens without releasing them back into your home.

Cordless vacuums are very effective at picking up pet hair, especially models designed with high suction power and specialized brush rolls. Some of the models we recommend include motorized attachments that lift embedded hair from upholstery and carpets. Anti-tangle vacuum heads are also a key feature that helps prevent hair from wrapping around the brush roll. While not all models perform equally, a high-quality cordless stick vacuum is able to handle pet hair just as well as traditional versions.

The lifespan of these vacuums typically lasts from three to five years, depending on usage, build quality, and maintenance. Battery performance plays a major role, since lithium-ion batteries start to lose capacity over time.

Avoiding overcharging and storing the vacuum properly could help extend the battery life significantly. Running regular maintenance like cleaning the vacuum filters and emptying the dustbin completely preserves suction and functionality. Higher-end models often last longer due to better materials and replaceable parts, but either price point has potential to last for years with proper care.

Modern designs are increasingly more effective at cleaning many surface types, including rugs and carpets. Advances in battery tech and general efficiency have allowed for strong suction performance that is comparable to corded models. Adjustable power modes allow for deeper cleaning on thicker carpets, and multi-roller and motorized brush heads agitate fibers to loosen dirt and debris.

Performance may vary depending on the type of flooring. Some vacuums are designed to work on low-to-medium pile carpets, while others are able to handle high-pile. It’s best to consult the listed features and specs of the model you’re considering to ensure it aligns with the flooring types in your home.

These 10 Brooks Running Shoes Will Help You Run Your Best Miles Yet

📅 2018-05-21  |  Read Source →

From the Ghost to the Glycerin, Brooks is our go-to brand for comfiest trainers.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

Editor’s Note, November 4, 2026: Brooks has a mega sale going on right now. The Glycerin Flex, Adrenaline GTS 25, and Glycerin Max 2 are on discounts as high as 25% off. The Adrenaline GTS 26 is out now—we’ll be getting in feedback soon. All this is to say, better stock up on the 25 before it’s gone.

In 1977, the Vantage was the first Brooks shoe to top our charts. The running renaissance began several years prior, when Frank Shorter won the 1972 Olympic marathon in Munich. Two years later, the shoe industry would be changed forever with the introduction of ethylene vinyl acetate (EVA) foam, which was lighter, softer, and had more rebound than the rubbers used in midsoles around that time. Our editors knew Brooks had a winner when we tested the Vantage.

Almost half a century later, the brand is still dazzling us with its innovations and earning prizes in the 2026 Runner’s World Shoe Awards. And to think the company started out more than a hundred years ago manufacturing ballet slippers and bath shoes.

Since the 1970s, Brooks has solely focused on upping the ante in shoe tech. The Vantage also introduced the varus wedge, which was basically a medial post used to help with instability caused by overpronation. Now the varus wedge is dated and has been replaced with Brooks’s “GuideRails” system. EVA is also being phased out with improved foams that are bouncier and even softer.

Gear Up for Training Season: Best Running Shorts ● Best Socks for Runners ● Best Running Watches

Our team vets hundreds of shoes a year and distributes models to more than 250 local testers for evaluation. Shoe testers of different levels, ages, arch heights, and gait patterns log at least a hundred miles before submitting feedback on a shoe’s fit, feel, and ride. But the Glycerin 23’s midsole upgrade convinced me to save my carbon shoes for race day. DNA Tuned is lighter than DNA Loft, the Glycerin’s previous foam, and delivers a springier ride. My legs feel fresh when I power down hills in the shoes. The energy return doesn’t make my double-digit runs feel like a crawl.

The shoe is also available as the Glycerin GTS 23, the stability counterpart. I find the Glycerin GTS 23 slightly stiffer and firmer than the neutral model, but it’s a good choice for runners who need a little more support on their long runs if they’re prone to late-onset overpronation, or if they prefer denser cushioning underfoot.

After adding a full PEBA midsole to the Hyperion Elite 5, Brooks zeroed in on everything else in the next iteration of the shoe. The Hyperion Elite 6 rides more aggressively thanks to its new geometry. The SpeedVault+ plate and RapidRoll rocker are adjusted to promote faster turnover, making those mid-race surges smooth—especially when you’re low on gas.

Our testers called out its accommodating fit in the midfoot area, saying it isn’t as narrow as other racing shoes they’ve tested. They appreciated the softness and comfort of the midsole as well as its propulsive bounce at marathon pace.

“This shoe is the most comfortable race day super shoe for maximizing performance in workouts and race scenarios in the half marathon distance or greater,” said tester Sean Phillips. “I found this shoe to be lighter, with slightly better grip on the rubber outsole.”

The Glycerin Flex changes the paradigm of cushioning as we know it. For one thing, its stack height is comparably low at 36mm for a cushioned trainer in a sea of high-stacked super trainers reaching beyond 40mm. The tooling on the midsole is also unlike anything we’ve seen. A diagonal midfoot Flex Zone allows you to bend the shoe in half in your hands. The pliability is meant to mimic how your foot is built, allowing your feet to flex instead of being forced to comply with stiff shoe construction.

The DNA Tuned cushioning is the same in the Glycerin Max and Glycerin 23. Small cells in the forefoot and large ones in the heel create a luxurious ride that absorbs shock without the foamy bulk. Lightweight and malleable, it challenges what we think when we hear cushioned trainer.

“The Brooks Glycerin Flex strikes an effective balance between lightweight construction, plush cushioning, flexible midsole, comfortable fit, and outsole durability, making it well suited as an everyday trainer,” said tester Lindsay Vuong. “The shoe transitions easily from running to gym sessions and daily wear. I especially appreciate the padded heel, which surrounds the back of my foot like a pillow while still holding it securely in place.”

The Adrenaline GTS turns 25, and it’s plusher and more responsive than its forebears. Just like the Ghost 17, Brooks added 1mm of cushioning to the heel and a whopping 3mm to the forefoot, making the shoe have a 10mm drop. This update packs it with more nitrogen-infused DNA Loft v3 cushioning and provides a more balanced platform.

Brooks’s guide rails on the lateral and medial sides of the shoe act as bumpers to align ankle and knee movement. This holistic approach to stability makes the shoe a great pick for overpronators and for neutral runners who need a little support later in their runs when their form gets sloppy This is why the Adrenaline is a long run favorite.

“I like that it felt like a normal trainer,” said Associate Service Editor Matt Rudisill, who runs with a neutral gait. “I thought these were going to be clunky and solid. I was pleasantly surprised that they were so comfortable, light, and still had that stable base that you want in a supportive shoe.”

Tester and overpronator Chris Garges also had praise for the Adrenaline GTS 25.

“I’ve run in just about every model of this shoe, many of them predating the guide rails system,” he said. “This shoe provides the stability that I need as a heel striking over pronator. I had no issues with the stability even later into a 14-mile training run.”

The Glycerin Max 2 provides a luxurious, max-cushioned ride. Testers who previously ran in the original Glycerin Max gave mixed feedback on the second model. They expected a squishy soft, relatively flexible high-stacked shoe. This version is stiffer; the shoe is meant for easy days and recovery miles.

“The cushioning, which is overkill 95 percent of the time, was quite pleasant on the slowest of slow days,” said tester Craig Wallace. “When my legs were tired and ready for an easy run, these shoes are a go-to.”

Matthew Fronheiser, a heel-striker, praised the Glycerin Max 2’s overall comfort, saying the upper “seriously rocks.” He liked its padded fit and breathability. He also appreciated the cushioned ride. The Glycerin Max 2 has Brooks’s DNA Tuned midsole foam and a GlideRoll profile, which promotes smooth turnover.

“Would I grab this shoe for a relaxed 8- to 10-mile run?” said Fronheiser. “One hundred times yes!”

Brooks added 4mm of nitrogen-infused DNA Loft v2 cushioning to the Cascadia 20 to provide even more protection and comfort running on the trail. Testers said the Vibram Megagrip outsole was a favorite feature, its 4mm lugs effectively bit into loose surfaces, handling mud and gravel well.

The Trail Adapt system—which involves a wide base, flexibility in key areas, and adaptable lugs—makes the Cascadia 20 a favorite shoe pick for runners who need more stability off-road.

A minor drawback: the lack of airflow. Testers found the shoe’s upper comfy but said the it ran hot. Despite the need for more ventilation, they described the Cascadia 20 as a solid trail shoe for most surfaces.

The shoe felt secure and reliable on uneven terrain, though it prioritizes durability and confidence over outright speed. Overall, it’s a versatile option for runners and hikers who want one shoe that can handle a wide range of trail adventures.”

Brooks has categorized its shoes to help runners choose the best pair that fits their running style and preferred experience. I’ve been a Brooks die-hard since my high school days. For the past 20 years, I’ve been keeping tabs on product updates and saying goodbye to discontinued lineups (taps for the PureProject minimalist list). Here’s a breakdown of everything you need to know—foams, stability tech—about Brooks’s footwear.

One helpful way to choose Brooks shoes is by knowing Brooks’s foams. The kind of foam determines the type of running experience the shoe is meant for.

Through the decades, Brooks revved up its EVA foam, developing MoGo foam in 2006, and then DNA foam in 2013. Known for its adaptive midsole cushioning, DNA has undergone an evolution of its own, breaking into its own subcategories in types of shoes.

Runners looking for a more cushioned shoe will want to try trainers with DNA Loft midsoles. DNA Loft v3, for example, which is featured in the Ghost 18 and the Adrenaline GTS 25, is nitrogen-infused EVA and rubber. It has large cells of air to pack in cushioning without adding extra weight.

At the beginning of 2025, Brooks released the Glycerin Max which showcased DNA Tuned. In developing DNA Tuned, the brand’s team found they could manipulate the cells creating larger cells in the heel and smaller cells in the forefoot. In an interview with Runner’s World, Brooks Chief Product Officer Carson Caprara explained how this dual-cell cushioning softens landings and becomes a springboard for responsive toe-off.

For runners looking for shoes for speedwork or racing, they should look for shoes with DNA Flash or DNA Gold; the latter is a pure PEBA midsole. DNA Hype is a combination of EVA and PEBA. We found in our testing its good for speedwork.

Before the Adrenaline GTS launched in 1999, Brooks had set out to build off of the success of the Addiction. Runners wanted a more flexible version of the stability shoe, and so, the Adrenaline GTS—Go-to-Shoe—was born.

In 2018, Brooks adapted a more holistic approach with stability, implementing a guide rails system into its shoes. The guide rails system has two dense foam pieces in the midsole, one on the medial side and one on the lateral side. These foam pieces reduce your foot from rolling outward and inward when your form becomes erratic due to overpronation or late onset overpronation.

Now redefined as Go-to-Support, GTS Brooks shoes are the stability counterpart of their neutral models. In this way, you can easily find your favorite Brooks trainer with just a little extra support in the midsole and not much else changed with the shoe.

Guide rails offer unobtrusive support for both overpronators and neutral runners.

Our lab tests show that DNA Loft remains Brooks’s softest midsole material.

Screen-printed mesh provides lightweight structure with more airflow and flexibility.

This sticky outsole compound aids grip and wet-surface traction on trail shoes.

The most responsive expression of Brooks’s DNA foam debuted on the Hyperion Tempo.

What’s the best Brooks shoe for beginning runners?

We always recommend the Ghost because it provides plenty of supportive cushioning without being too bulky. It’s an ideal daily trainer for beginners to start with as an offshoot.

How does the Hyperion Elite 6 compare to other carbon shoes?

According to testers, the Hyperion Elite 6 feels less softer and more accommodating compared to other super shoes they’ve tested. The new DNA Gold midsole adds more edge compared to the Hyperion Elite’s DNA Flash foams, with testers saying the rebound is comparable to the Adidas Adizero Adios Pro 4 or Saucony Endorphin Pro 5.

Does Brooks have bargain-priced shoes and how do they stack up to more expensive models?

The Launch 12, included on this list, has a fit and feel almost on par with more expensive models. It’s not as responsive compared to $140 to $160 trainers due to its EVA cushioning, but it delivers a smooth ride.

Crock-Pot Chicken Noodle Soup

📅 2017-09-21  |  Read Source →

The pinnacle of cozy, comforting food is chicken noodle soup. When you need something warming and satisfying, there’s just nothing better. While our classic recipe is already easy to whip up, tossing all of your ingredients in the slow cooker makes this soup easier than ever. After a few hours and very little effort, you’ll have the coziest meal on your hands, ready to warm you up all fall and winter long.

What People Are Saying:“Perfect for snow days. Easy to make and there is no wrong time for soup” - evabnyc2372

“ Very easy....very good thank god I made a big pot bc I have 3 boys my oldest had 5 servings and my youngest had two giant servings my middle son had 3” - ashleybaby3586

The best thing about this recipe? It’s not necessary to cook the chicken before putting it in the soup—let your slow cooker do all the hard work for you. Place the boneless, skinless chicken breasts in the slow cooker, along with the veggies, herbs, butter, and broth. Season with salt and pepper, then cook on high with the lid closed for 2 1/2 hours.

By now, the chicken should be cooked and tender. Transfer it to a plate, and after letting it cool slightly, use 2 forks to shred it (or whatever is your favorite shredding method).

Discard the thyme, rosemary, and bay leaf from the slow cooker, then add the shredded chicken back in along with the egg noodles. Cover and cook on High for about 5 to 10 minutes—you're looking for the noodles to be al dente.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

If you have leftovers, I strongly suggest that you store the noodles separately from the broth. If they’re stored together, the noodles will soak up the liquid and turn to mush, and there won’t be any broth left. If you’d like to cook this soup in advance, the same recommendation applies, though if you’re making it ahead of time, I'd recommend skipping the noodles completely. You can add them to the broth when you reheat the soup, and they should be fully cooked in just 5 minutes.

Same deal if you're freezing—the soup itself freezes well and can be reheated whenever the craving hits, but the noodles will get mushy sitting in all of that broth, so I'd recommend cooking them off fresh!
